    <div class="article-content">
    <div class="widget widget-ArticleFulltext widget-instance-articleFulltext_Minimal">
        <div class="module-widget">
    <div class="widget-items" data-widgetname="ArticleFulltext">





                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922626 id="223922626" class="abstract-title js-splitscreen-abstract-title" >Abstract</h2>
<section class="abstract" aria-label="Main abstract"><div class=" sec"><div class="title">Context</div><p class="chapter-para">Arterial hypertension (AHT) is one of the most frequent pathologies in the general population. Subtypes of essential hypertension characterized by low renin levels allowed the identification of 2 different clinical entities: aldosterone-mediated mineralocorticoid receptor (MR) activation and cortisol-mediated MR activation.</p></div><div class=" sec"><div class="title">Evidence Acquisition</div><p class="chapter-para">This review is based upon a search of Pubmed and Google Scholar databases, up to August 2019, for all publications relating to endocrine hypertension, apparent mineralocorticoid excess (AME) and cortisol (F) to cortisone (E) metabolism.</p></div><div class=" sec"><div class="title">Evidence Synthesis</div><p class="chapter-para">The spectrum of cortisol-mediated MR activation includes the classic AME syndrome to milder (nonclassic) forms of AME, the latter with a much higher prevalence (7.1%) than classic AME but different phenotype and genotype. Nonclassic AME (NC-AME) is mainly related to partial 11βHSD2 deficiency associated with genetic variations and epigenetic modifications (first hit) and potential additive actions of endogenous or exogenous inhibitors (ie, glycyrrhetinic acid-like factors [GALFS]) and other factors (ie, age, high sodium intake) (second hit). Subjects with NC-AME are characterized by a high F/E ratio, low E levels, normal to elevated blood pressure, low plasma renin and increased urinary potassium excretion. NC-AME condition should benefit from low-sodium and potassium diet recommendations and monotherapy with MR antagonists.</p></div><div class=" sec"><div class="title">Conclusion</div><p class="chapter-para">NC-AME has a higher prevalence and a milder phenotypical spectrum than AME. NC-AME etiology is associated to a first hit (gene and epigene level) and an additive second hit. NC-AME subjects are candidates to be treated with MR antagonists aimed to improve blood pressure, end-organ damage, and modulate the renin levels.</p></div></section>                    <div class="article-metadata-panel clearfix at-ArticleMetadata"></div>
<div class="kwd-group"><a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="AME">AME</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;nonclassic AME&quot;">nonclassic AME</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;arterial hypertension&quot;">arterial hypertension</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;low renin&quot;">low renin</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="metabolic">metabolic</a></div><p class="chapter-para">Arterial hypertension (AHT) is a multifactorial disease with a complex pathogenesis that occurs from the interplay between genetic, metabolic and environmental factors that lead to changes in the blood pressure (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0001 CIT0002 CIT0003"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0001 CIT0002 CIT0003" data-open="CIT0001 CIT0002 CIT0003" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1–3</a>). Arterial hypertension is a major risk factor for stroke, myocardial infarction, heart failure, and end-stage renal disease.</p><p class="chapter-para">Worldwide, the prevalence of AHT in adults over 25 years of age is 25% (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0004"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0004" data-open="CIT0004" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">4</a>), contributing to 45% to 50% of deaths due to heart disease and stroke (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0005"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0005" data-open="CIT0005" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">5</a>,<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0006"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0006" data-open="CIT0006" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">6</a>), making AHT a major concern for public health, particularly in Western countries (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0007 CIT0008 CIT0009 CIT0010 CIT0011 CIT0012"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0007 CIT0008 CIT0009 CIT0010 CIT0011 CIT0012" data-open="CIT0007 CIT0008 CIT0009 CIT0010 CIT0011 CIT0012" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">7–12</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">However, to date, most cases of AHT do not identify a specific etiology and are misclassified as essential hypertension. Over the last 20 years, we and others have studied a subtype of essential hypertension characterized by low renin levels, allowing for the identification of 2 different mechanisms: aldosterone-mediated mineralocorticoid receptor (MR) activation associated with primary aldosteronism (PA) (with a reported prevalence of 5–10%) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0013"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0013" data-open="CIT0013" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">13</a>) and cortisol-mediated MR activation, highlighting the apparent mineralocorticoid excess (AME) syndrome and a milder form of AME recently named nonclassic AME (NC-AME), which presented a prevalence of around 7% (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0014"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0014" data-open="CIT0014" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">14</a>).</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922631 id="223922631" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s1>Mineralocorticoid-dependent Hypertension</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Mineralocorticoid-dependent hypertension represents one of the most prevalent causes of secondary hypertension. Pathological conditions associated with aldosterone-mediated MR activation and cortisol-mediated MR activation are responsible for the etiology of AHT in up to 15% of AHT patients. Both conditions are mostly related to an unregulated activation of the MR. The activation of the MR by aldosterone or cortisol is a key step in the pathological process leading to mineralocorticoid hypertension (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0015"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0015" data-open="CIT0015" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">15</a>), since both can increase sodium transport in renal collecting ducts, increasing water uptake, blood volume, and secondary raising of blood pressure (BP). Aldosterone-MR and cortisol-MR complexes exert important roles in water-electrolyte homeostasis in the epithelial tissues of the kidneys and colon and also in nonepithelial tissues (ie, heart, brain) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0016"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0016" data-open="CIT0016" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">16</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0017"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0017" data-open="CIT0017" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">17</a>) through genomic and nongenomic actions.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922633 id="223922633" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s2>Lessons From Primary Aldosteronism</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Primary aldosteronism (PA) is a condition in which the biosynthesis of aldosterone is increased, independent of renin and sodium status, leading to AHT, cardiovascular damage, heart diseases (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0018 CIT0019 CIT0020"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0018 CIT0019 CIT0020" data-open="CIT0018 CIT0019 CIT0020" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">18–20</a>), and renal and immune system alterations (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0021 CIT0022 CIT0023 CIT0024 CIT0025"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0021 CIT0022 CIT0023 CIT0024 CIT0025" data-open="CIT0021 CIT0022 CIT0023 CIT0024 CIT0025" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">21–25</a>). Primary aldosteronism is considered an uncommon disease that is suspected in patients with severe hypertension and hypokalemia. However, several studies have shown that PA is a prevalent condition, showing a prevalence of around 5% in the general hypertensive population and of around 10% in reference centers (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0013"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0013" data-open="CIT0013" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">13</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0026"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0026" data-open="CIT0026" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">26</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0027"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0027" data-open="CIT0027" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">27</a>), which is detected by measurements of the serum aldosterone-to-renin ratio (ARR) as the screening tool and further confirmatory tests (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0013"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0013" data-open="CIT0013" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">13</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0028 CIT0029 CIT0030 CIT0031"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0028 CIT0029 CIT0030 CIT0031" data-open="CIT0028 CIT0029 CIT0030 CIT0031" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">28–31</a>). Currently, the prevalence of PA is dependent on the population being targeted, the ARR threshold for positive screen test, and the confirmatory test performed (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0032"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0032" data-open="CIT0032" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">32</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0033"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0033" data-open="CIT0033" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">33</a>). Primary aldosteronism can be commonly caused by an adrenal adenoma, unilateral or bilateral adrenal hyperplasia (BAH), or in rare cases by an adrenal carcinoma. The majority of cases are sporadic, but PA can be transmitted as an inherited condition of familial hyperaldosteronism (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0015"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0015" data-open="CIT0015" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">15</a>). According to the Endocrine Society guidelines, in presence of a family history of PA or early-onset hypertension further genetics studies are recommended (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0015"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0015" data-open="CIT0015" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">15</a>). Here, at least 4 forms of familial hyperaldosteronism (FH) have been reported: FH-I (glucocorticoid-remediable aldosteronism [GRA]), which displays a severe phenotype with early-onset hypertension, and the estimated prevalence is only 1% of PA subjects and is secondary to a chimeric gene (<em>CYP11B1</em>/<em>CYP11B2</em>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0013"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0013" data-open="CIT0013" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">13</a>); FH-II, which has been described in 6% of the familial form of PA associated with gain-of-function mutations in the <em>CLCN2</em> gene coding for the CLC-2 chloride channel (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0034 CIT0035 CIT0036"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0034 CIT0035 CIT0036" data-open="CIT0034 CIT0035 CIT0036" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">34–36</a>); FH-III, which was described secondary to a gain-of-function germline mutation in the <em>KCNJ5</em> gene (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0037"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0037" data-open="CIT0037" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">37</a>); and FH-IV, which is caused by a heterozygous mutation in the <em>CACNA1H</em> gene (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0038"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0038" data-open="CIT0038" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">38</a>) (OMIM: 607904) on chromosome 16p13.</p><p class="chapter-para">Currently, the importance of diagnosing PA has become clear, not only for the deleterious consequences of high BP but also because of the direct deleterious effects of MR activation, independent of the BP in several organs and tissues, such as those of the cardiovascular, renal, and immune system as well as adipose tissue. The deleterious effects of PA on the cardiovascular system include myocardial fibrosis, a reduction in fibrinolysis, and endothelial dysfunction (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0039"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0039" data-open="CIT0039" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">39</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0040"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0040" data-open="CIT0040" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">40</a>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F1"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F1" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 1</a>). Similarly, cell signaling by the aldosterone/MR complex is a key mediator of kidney injury and renal dysfunction (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0041"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0041" data-open="CIT0041" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">41</a>) identified by high levels of urinary albuminuria and creatinine. Aldosterone also has been associated to changes in the immune system (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0042"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0042" data-open="CIT0042" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">42</a>) and adipose tissue metabolism (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0043"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0043" data-open="CIT0043" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">43</a>), promoting an inflammatory state characterized by a vascular infiltration of immune cells, reactive oxidative stress, and proinflammatory cytokine production (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0042"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0042" data-open="CIT0042" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">42</a>).</p>                    <a id="223922636" scrollto-destination="223922636"></a>
<div data-id="f1" data-content-id="f1" class="fig fig-section js-fig-section" swap-content-for-modal="true"><div class="graphic-wrap"><img class="content-image" src="https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/jcem/105/4/10.1210_clinem_dgz315/5/m_dgz315f0001.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&amp;Signature=tXq~JSV2~xG6U8Hx73QBmc0e-IjoF7gR2Bxy38zmyweCiucnf7-iq~uo76kTez0ENQryeqAhdtNk9M~-QLou7tLGl-vsB4Fbh10BdNja~YRc6L8Diznk8c9sUXgTdU09wVOx-lYy0jmgrUBnS6vhiyuQZ8T4aTT48iY51GKE1q3DQi8ZIe1c8-fVLW5fzBZitalw43KBqtt2pYBDRgMZCo1B7~7NmX3x3cVHDERVYe-pw~DAaQ7ucaRB7ET8lqL2XR7mA3r67t7sLaF-J8RRM6gXDb3-DLdMPjW4uqAfkRMM3GtmWQNQ47S6AjNZLZDnii967seRXJkUEVwVo87jlw__&amp;Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA" alt="Renal and extrarenal deleterious effects by increased aldosterone or cortisol-mediated MR activation. Aldosterone and cortisol are able to activate MR in epithelial and nonepithelial tissues. In 11βHSD2 deficiency or inhibition, cortisol can bind MR in epithelial tissue, and induce MR activation, similar to aldosterone. Unregulated MR activation induces deleterious effects in the kidney and probably in other tissues, such as the heart, blood vessels, and the immune system." data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0001.jpg" /><div class="graphic-bottom"><div class="label fig-label" id="label-223922636">Figure 1.</div><div class="caption fig-caption"><p class="chapter-para">Renal and extrarenal deleterious effects by increased aldosterone or cortisol-mediated MR activation. Aldosterone and cortisol are able to activate MR in epithelial and nonepithelial tissues. In 11βHSD2 deficiency or inhibition, cortisol can bind MR in epithelial tissue, and induce MR activation, similar to aldosterone. Unregulated MR activation induces deleterious effects in the kidney and probably in other tissues, such as the heart, blood vessels, and the immune system.</p></div><div class="ajax-articleAbstract-exclude-regex fig-orig original-slide figure-button-wrap"><a class="fig-view-orig js-view-large at-figureViewLarge openInAnotherWindow" role="button" aria-describedby="label-223922636" href="/view-large/figure/223922636/dgz315f0001.jpg" data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0001.jpg" target="_blank">Open in new tab</a><a class="download-slide" role="button" aria-describedby="label-223922636" data-section="223922636" href="/DownloadFile/DownloadImage.aspx?image=https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/jcem/105/4/10.1210_clinem_dgz315/5/dgz315f0001.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&Signature=dRDHUuaq7eTVFUTvsT8J-KOPGBoxvBlF8tCVDu1xtfruWGx4GGkLONOYNypJoOcJKcO7tSLPg5ghgS7scGpcsiAmQXsBnl~u~W9hYzRXqBWX4lL4Eyf8UndIvJ7kO9pmVWNRznMbcSXfHJ-iLv56oA~hfNxD2LKIGpJlMfD~NhwTLr8sfxarBLA3sPyVAxkptfBwkhLHfJUjA7tc6AUFJiBTlmjSruuutzpElJYiHWRnra0fwpDymTNZS0rztPLjsFEBXcenWRarwMWCo70XeCfjtgQyGQOFHcRlH7jwV3n~jIK-Sq9tBFcA-YlvVLxyveuosWHyTNZ6U1~p9jcWXQ__&Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA&sec=223922636&ar=5691192&xsltPath=~/UI/app/XSLT&imagename=&siteId=5591" data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0001.jpg">Download slide</a></div></div></div></div><p class="chapter-para">Recently, Brown et al (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0044"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0044" data-open="CIT0044" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">44</a>) performed a longitudinal cohort study using physiological phenotypes of autonomous aldosterone secretion and MR activity. In this study, they proposed that a broader spectrum of clinically relevant renin-independent aldosteronism (PA) may extend to subjects with no apparent clinical symptoms of excessive MR activation by aldosterone and even normotensives. Moreover, they suggest that a proportion of cases of hypertension may not be “essential” (or idiopathic) and could be associated with MR-mediated hypertension as a pathogenic mechanism, potentially treated with MR antagonists.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922638 id="223922638" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s3>Apparent Mineralocorticoid Excess Syndrome</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Apparent mineralocorticoid excess syndrome (OMIM: 218030), also called classic AME, is a rare autosomal recessive disorder caused by the presence of a severe deficiency of 11β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase type 2 (11βHSD2) activity, mainly due to multiple pathogenic variants in the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0045 CIT0046 CIT0047"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0045 CIT0046 CIT0047" data-open="CIT0045 CIT0046 CIT0047" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">45–47</a>). The clinical features are childhood-onset hypertension, hypokalemia, and alkalosis with low plasma renin, but unlike PA, AME displays low aldosterone levels (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0045"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0045" data-open="CIT0045" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">45</a>) in the presence of a high serum or urinary cortisol/cortisone (F/E) ratio (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0048"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0048" data-open="CIT0048" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">48</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0049"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0049" data-open="CIT0049" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">49</a>). This syndrome is characterized by the increased chronic agonistic action of cortisol on the MR, which leads primarily to increased renal sodium and water reabsorption, leading to AHT (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0045"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0045" data-open="CIT0045" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">45</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0050"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0050" data-open="CIT0050" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">50</a>), and secondarily to an impaired vascular response, endothelial oxidative stress and subclinical inflammation.</p><p class="chapter-para">Cortisol can bind to the MR with equal affinity to aldosterone (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0051"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0051" data-open="CIT0051" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">51</a>) and then in physiopathological conditions cortisol can induce the same effects as does aldosterone over MR (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0051 CIT0052 CIT0053"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0051 CIT0052 CIT0053" data-open="CIT0051 CIT0052 CIT0053" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">51–53</a>). This is particularly relevant in human physiology, since the concentrations of circulating cortisol are 1000 to 2000 times higher than those of aldosterone, the natural substrate of the MR. Under normal conditions, the activation of the MR by cortisol does not occur because the enzyme 11βHSD2 (NG_016549) efficiently inactivates cortisol (F) to cortisone (E), avoiding the binding of cortisol to MR (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0002"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0002" data-open="CIT0002" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0047"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0047" data-open="CIT0047" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">47</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0054"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0054" data-open="CIT0054" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">54</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0055"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0055" data-open="CIT0055" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">55</a>). A severe deficiency of 11βHSD2 activity triggers severe hypertension associated with an early suppression of plasmatic renin levels (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0045"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0045" data-open="CIT0045" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">45</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0046"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0046" data-open="CIT0046" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">46</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0056 CIT0057 CIT0058"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0056 CIT0057 CIT0058" data-open="CIT0056 CIT0057 CIT0058" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">56–58</a>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-T1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="T1" data-open="T1" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">Table 1</a>).</p>                    <a id="223922641" scrollto-destination="223922641"></a>
<div content-id="T1" class="table-modal table-full-width-wrap"><div class="table-wrap table-wide standard-table"><div class="table-wrap-title" id="T1" data-id="T1"><span class="label title-label" id="label-44357">Table 1.</span><div class="&#xA;                            graphic-wrap table-open-button-wrap&#xA;                            "><a class="fig-view-orig at-tableViewLarge openInAnotherWindow btn js-view-large" role="button" target="_blank" href="&#xA;                                /view-large/223922641" aria-describedby="label-44357">

5α-ring A-reduced derivatives of corticosterone and 11β-hydroxy-progesterone). 4. Regulators of 11βHSD2: GR, RAC1-GTPase, SUMOylation, hedgehog signaling.</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div pressure</td><td>High</td><td>Normal—elevated</td></tr><tr><td>Nephrocalcinosis</td><td>Present</td><td>Absent</td></tr><tr><td>Left ventricular hypertrophy</td><td>Present</td><td>Unknown</td></tr><tr><td>Growth rate</td><td>-2 to -3 SD (&lt;5th percentile)</td><td>Most likely, normal</td></tr><tr><td>Serum potassium</td><td>Very low</td><td>Normal/low</td></tr><tr><td>Urinary Na<sup>+</sup>/K<sup>+</sup></td><td>Very low</td><td>Low</td></tr><tr><td>TTKG</td><td>High</td><td>Normal/high</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Serum Hormones</strong></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td> Serum aldosterone</td><td>Very low</td><td>Normal/low</td></tr><tr><td> Plasma renin activity</td><td>Very low—undetectable</td><td>Low</td></tr><tr><td> Cortisol</td><td>Normal</td><td>Normal</td></tr><tr><td> Cortisone</td><td>Very low (&lt;2.5th percentile)</td><td>Low (&lt;25th Hormones</strong></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td> Cortisol (F)</td><td>Normal</td><td>Normal</td></tr><tr><td> Cortisone (E)</td><td>Very low</td><td>Low</td></tr><tr><td> Cortisol to cortisone ratio (F/E)</td><td>Very high</td><td>High</td></tr><tr><td> Tetrahydrocortisol (THF)</td><td>Normal</td><td>Normal</td></tr><tr><td> Tetrahydrocortisone (THE)</td><td>Very (1–6%)</td><td>Inferred low (40–60% activity from normal)</td></tr><tr><td>Etiology</td><td>1. Pathogenic variations in <em>HSD11B2</em> gene (homozygous recessive or compound heterozygous).</td><td>1. Variations in <em>HSD11B2</em> gene (heterozygous). 2. Epigenetic deleterious condition, miRNA action. 3. Exogenous/endogenous chemical inhibition (ie, licorice, GALFs, 5α-ring A-reduced derivatives of corticosterone and 11β-hydroxy-progesterone). 4. Regulators of 11βHSD2: GR, RAC1-GTPase, SUMOylation, hedgehog signaling.</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div low—undetectable</td><td>Low</td></tr><tr><td> Cortisol</td><td>Normal</td><td>Normal</td></tr><tr><td> Cortisone</td><td>Very low (&lt;2.5th percentile)</td><td>Low (&lt;25th percentile)</td></tr><tr><td> Cortisol to cortisone (F/E)</td><td>Very high (&gt;97.5th percentile)</td><td>High (&gt;75th percentile)</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Urine ratio (F/E)</td><td>Very high</td><td>High</td></tr><tr><td> Tetrahydrocortisol (THF)</td><td>Normal</td><td>Normal</td></tr><tr><td> Tetrahydrocortisone (THE)</td><td>Very (1–6%)</td><td>Inferred low (40–60% activity from normal)</td></tr><tr><td>Etiology</td><td>1. Pathogenic variations in <em>HSD11B2</em> gene (homozygous recessive or compound 5α-ring A-reduced derivatives of corticosterone and 11β-hydroxy-progesterone). 4. Regulators of 11βHSD2: GR, RAC1-GTPase, SUMOylation, hedgehog signaling.</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div class="table-wrap-foot"><span id="fn-fn-0100"></span><div content-id="fn-0100" class="footnote"><span class="fn"><p class="chapter-para">Abbreviations: GALFs, glycyrrhetinic acid-like factors; TTKG, transtubular potassium gradient.</p></span></div></div></div></div><p class="chapter-para">Following the description of the first disease-causing pathogenic variation, approximately 40 additional germline mutations have been reported (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0047"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0047" data-open="CIT0047" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">47</a>), mainly in families where consanguineous marriages, endogamy, or a founder effect occurred. The recent development of a computational model of the 11βHSD2 protein provided a structural explanation for the genotype–phenotype correlation and the clinical severity of AME syndrome resulting from the reported mutations (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0045 CIT0046 CIT0047"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0045 CIT0046 CIT0047" data-open="CIT0045 CIT0046 CIT0047" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">45–47</a>). In addition, the same model can be used to predict disease severity for novel described missense mutations in the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0047"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0047" data-open="CIT0047" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">47</a>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>). Apparent mineralocorticoid excess reports describe point mutations in intronic and exonic regions of the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene in approximately 70 families around the world, most of them partially or totally affecting the activity of the 11βHSD2 enzyme (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0132"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0132" data-open="CIT0132" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">132</a>). Furthermore, the presence of polymorphisms, variations in microsatellite regions (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0059"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0059" data-open="CIT0059" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">59</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0060"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0060" data-open="CIT0060" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">60</a>) and epigenetic modifications in the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene can affect its expression (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0061"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0061" data-open="CIT0061" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">61</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0062"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0062" data-open="CIT0062" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">62</a>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>). Affected subjects with classic AME display a low birth weight, severe hypertension, nephrocalcinosis, hypokalemia, suppressed renin, and low aldosterone (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0046"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0046" data-open="CIT0046" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">46</a>). Biochemically, subjects with AME have a markedly elevated serum F/E ratio (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0046"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0046" data-open="CIT0046" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">46</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0063"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0063" data-open="CIT0063" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">63</a>), and urinary tetrahydrocortisol (THF) + alloTHF to tetrahydrocortisone (THE) ratio [(THF + alloTHF)/THE] (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0064 CIT0065 CIT0066"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0064 CIT0065 CIT0066" data-open="CIT0064 CIT0065 CIT0066" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">64–66</a>) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-T1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="T1" data-open="T1" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">Table 1</a>).</p>                    <a id="223922643" scrollto-destination="223922643"></a>
<div data-id="f2" data-content-id="f2" class="fig fig-section js-fig-section" swap-content-for-modal="true"><div class="graphic-wrap"><img class="content-image" src="https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/jcem/105/4/10.1210_clinem_dgz315/5/m_dgz315f0002.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&amp;Signature=n3PkRA2hy1sJBnMUQPIzzz3N6PYa2-ZjgdTDNVxk0ahfyH7-kJHF8HcwENwhCKBhAFpcZNRf8UpMVNZIz-P02PT7MIU7lvJPZPXfMpjMMLAlY0500Trvt9KwYdlX6UQLPRzQXfRIgJAu~CN0LnQScyu9-JhqZjrqNkwPfOqksDwSvUvBQW1WAXSERE4zaXqDTpg5vnUEtCasG0l7bjES3EFnVwBzTvQ9JPGCWpD4PkLy~OwnWsxW8-CLoONexe3kSFxJoS9bcMCvpp79hsz4cry7CggUBmT6sB5lM5~Pctm64rf-lxzVxJIEN46m48o6DUNU40AM5lEq-rpXxIoz8A__&amp;Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA" alt="Genetic variants in the human HSD11B2 gene. The diagram shows the human HSD11B2 gene and the location of identified pathogenic variants causing AME. The HSD11B2 gene is located on chromosome 16 and contains 5 exons, 4 CpG regions, 2 main (CA) repeat regions, and a promoter with SP1/SP3, NF1, NF-κB, and EGR-1 sites. Exons are represented by gray boxes. Mutations are listed relative to their position in the gene." data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0002.jpg" /><div class="graphic-bottom"><div class="label fig-label" id="label-223922643">Figure 2.</div><div class="caption fig-caption"><p class="chapter-para">Genetic variants in the human <em>HSD11B2</em> gene. The diagram shows the human <em>HSD11B2</em> gene and the location of identified pathogenic variants causing AME. The <em>HSD11B2</em> gene is located on chromosome 16 and contains 5 exons, 4 CpG regions, 2 main (CA) repeat regions, and a promoter with SP1/SP3, NF1, NF-κB, and EGR-1 sites. Exons are represented by gray boxes. Mutations are listed relative to their position in the gene.</p></div><div class="ajax-articleAbstract-exclude-regex fig-orig original-slide figure-button-wrap"><a class="fig-view-orig js-view-large at-figureViewLarge openInAnotherWindow" role="button" aria-describedby="label-223922643" href="/view-large/figure/223922643/dgz315f0002.jpg" data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0002.jpg" target="_blank">Open in new tab</a><a class="download-slide" role="button" aria-describedby="label-223922643" data-section="223922643" href="/DownloadFile/DownloadImage.aspx?image=https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/jcem/105/4/10.1210_clinem_dgz315/5/dgz315f0002.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&Signature=vXA8ucIJCtgtTZDKaKPOG6iT~At4faPkA4UlWbPeS2qCvKRq9nWe7QVQBnVvc4iikBzC-9OdQhgDnmHB3l7Wp784sPFut6VpivJFPVrN~X2rIE0JOoY~fPZpWJ8R819F5V~J8jj06KgK8zyWqOJnMB4lwOb26Vm~7DaH9ZjD1MfxXEQDXjkjGArhWZaq-s1TQdyUM0V2XP~z8Rk7MH3sMeKhMCJFSigotrfb6PYcgXDZzSR749UEBvejKeFFCVJTojW-nXzcBpE5zP20hZP4iagFtHQVf1YDccNGJBYvQTn1Bd6nNcboarG5DMVxHQlVmk6Yidn67olXUPJUSS9WzQ__&Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA&sec=223922643&ar=5691192&xsltPath=~/UI/app/XSLT&imagename=&siteId=5591" data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0002.jpg">Download slide</a></div></div></div></div><p class="chapter-para">Clinically, the patients affected by AME syndrome respond well to MR antagonists (as the activation of the MR is implicated in the pathogenesis of the disease) and to dexamethasone, which reduces adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)-mediated cortisol production. Although classic AME syndrome is rare (<a class="link link-uri openInAnotherWindow" href="https://rarediseases.info.nih.gov/diseases/433/apparent-mineralocorticoid-excess" target="_blank">https://rarediseases.info.nih.gov/diseases/433/apparent-mineralocorticoid-excess</a>), recent research suggests that the spectrum of cortisol-mediated MR activation is broader than currently recognized, and milder forms of AME could be present in the general population (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0014"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0014" data-open="CIT0014" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">14</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0067"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0067" data-open="CIT0067" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">67</a>), for which hypertensive patients could be mislabeled as idiopathic.</p><p class="chapter-para">For instance, Ulick et al described a milder version of AME and named it the type 2 variant, caused by a decrease in the cortisol clearance rate (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0068"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0068" data-open="CIT0068" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">68</a>). More recently, cross-sectional human studies have shown that both cortisol and the cortisol/cortisone ratio increased with age, but cortisone levels and renin activity declined with older age, suggesting a decrease in 11βHSD2 activity (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0069"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0069" data-open="CIT0069" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">69</a>). Since a higher F/E ratio and the low-renin phenotype have been correlated with higher BP in both adults and children (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0063"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0063" data-open="CIT0063" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">63</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0070"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0070" data-open="CIT0070" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">70</a>), new evidence suggests that a proportion of low-renin hypertension may be explained by a less severe, or “nonclassic,” phenotype of AME that may respond to MR antagonists.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922646 id="223922646" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s4>Nonclassic Apparent Mineralocorticoid Excess (NC-AME)</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Nonclassic apparent mineralocorticoid excess is proposed as a novel clinical condition with milder phenotypical spectrum than identified for AME (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0014"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0014" data-open="CIT0014" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">14</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0067"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0067" data-open="CIT0067" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">67</a>), and similar to the recently proposed spectrum for PA (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0014"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0014" data-open="CIT0014" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">14</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0033"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0033" data-open="CIT0033" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">33</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0044"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0044" data-open="CIT0044" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">44</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0046"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0046" data-open="CIT0046" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">46</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0047"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0047" data-open="CIT0047" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">47</a>) that ranges from normotension to severe AHT (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F3"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F3" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 3</a>). In the literature, the mild form of AME has been described previously as AME type II (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0068"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0068" data-open="CIT0068" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">68</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0071"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0071" data-open="CIT0071" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">71</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0072"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0072" data-open="CIT0072" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">72</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0133"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0133" data-open="CIT0133" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">133</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0134"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0134" data-open="CIT0134" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">134</a>). Apparent mineralocorticoid excess type II refers to subjects with a low-renin phenotype, subtle changes in the urinary ratio of (THF+5aTHF)/THE, and decreased clearance of cortisol. In agreement with Li and Stewart (1998) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0134"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0134" data-open="CIT0134" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">134</a>), the phenotype of AME type II should be seen as a continuum, explained on the basis of mutations in the human <em>HSD11B2</em> gene that have varying degrees of “severity” (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F3"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F3" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 3</a>). Twenty years after this report, there is clinical and biochemical evidence that support this continuum phenotype, which could be affected by genetics, as epigenetics, sodium, and environmental factors.</p>                    <a id="223922648" scrollto-destination="223922648"></a>
<div data-id="f3" data-content-id="f3" class="fig fig-section js-fig-section" swap-content-for-modal="true"><div class="graphic-wrap"><img class="content-image" src="https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/jcem/105/4/10.1210_clinem_dgz315/5/m_dgz315f0003.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&amp;Signature=pT~NEXCa52K~zNpvSohj9OEh8GAK~uh~zNicit5hKtV9QiUIl4m8EXDZKCNqBA6IFZO8pJeSSg3k7K0BPlvq6gueoV~P8ivSqtzAS26~AtSSUICalwNbDel7VSu4PzCBf81NkhM4vxTZb~7x5no~MIaC9xqAfJ4AG3Vm2AnfqGKKcHiAV3JfWGjCcr-bw4iBpHUmB1Vqo7FJK68Nwv~TDjmJuGwXM0HITlnVDeVZi3hoZlOtIsJCi0EQaaIKR3oUx72kSt3FOistuSjlkiiVQ0jaFPcSgD~SCAQr2UhJajRMRurLkuPSXm4~e1d-HEaJLm4FmkIW1bdPRHRw54~Bfw__&amp;Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA" alt="Phenotypical spectrum of AME and nonclassic AME. Classic AME and nonclassic AME are shown as a continuous spectrum. The scheme highlight the clinical and biochemical presentation of both conditions associated with first and second hits. Nonclassic apparent mineralocorticoid excess is a phenotype mainly related to an 11βHSD2 deficiency associated with genetic and epigenetic modifications affecting the HSD11B2 gene expression (first hit) and the potential additive action of endogenous or exogenous inhibitors (ie, GALFS) (second hit). Treatment for NC-AME and AME is also shown. Abbreviations: BP, blood pressure; GALFs, glycyrrhetinic acid-like factors; IUGR, intrauterine growth restriction; LVH, left ventricular hypertrophy; NR3C1, human glucocorticoid receptor (GR) gene." data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0003.jpg" /><div class="graphic-bottom"><div class="label fig-label" id="label-223922648">Figure 3.</div><div class="caption fig-caption"><p class="chapter-para">Phenotypical spectrum of AME and nonclassic AME. Classic AME and nonclassic AME are shown as a continuous spectrum. The scheme highlight the clinical and biochemical presentation of both conditions associated with first and second hits. Nonclassic apparent mineralocorticoid excess is a phenotype mainly related to an 11βHSD2 deficiency associated with genetic and epigenetic modifications affecting the HSD11B2 gene expression (first hit) and the potential additive action of endogenous or exogenous inhibitors (ie, GALFS) (second hit). Treatment for NC-AME and AME is also shown. Abbreviations: BP, blood pressure; GALFs, glycyrrhetinic acid-like factors; IUGR, intrauterine growth restriction; LVH, left ventricular hypertrophy; NR3C1, human glucocorticoid receptor (GR) gene.</p></div><div class="ajax-articleAbstract-exclude-regex fig-orig original-slide figure-button-wrap"><a class="fig-view-orig js-view-large at-figureViewLarge openInAnotherWindow" role="button" aria-describedby="label-223922648" href="/view-large/figure/223922648/dgz315f0003.jpg" data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0003.jpg" target="_blank">Open in new tab</a><a class="download-slide" role="button" aria-describedby="label-223922648" data-section="223922648" href="/DownloadFile/DownloadImage.aspx?image=https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/jcem/105/4/10.1210_clinem_dgz315/5/dgz315f0003.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&Signature=iTWKBAglS0Bka63ybVxWxC3queklDnpfpXBy6WNoFYddYsMO-gPJVRVk0eIDHT~N0hH3AKA4LAUD-d3bjXta~k3iO3yKduLkTNMJ-N6moJHs7rmCItDAEOt7ZdwMKE9oZYkIOLB8m~fR5ZGvc~pX~qFDS1grt5cY7O6U~SRYrunhlAFk04x1NVN9iZH55LzB6xIuHe4fAw9Bk4h9y10~J-TQZ9JK8yX-fnhM16i6opMz30Z-aAUyd-qFdyFrZ9dqFxEO8fUmmdfQS6sqzb6E5GN7PaZmJorJZTUOloHJdEroKzkuxkv12VJKthLk-rQWT9uZMHqYux2yf7yLAJf5GA__&Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA&sec=223922648&ar=5691192&xsltPath=~/UI/app/XSLT&imagename=&siteId=5591" data-path-from-xml="dgz315f0003.jpg">Download slide</a></div></div></div></div><p class="chapter-para">The NC-AME condition can be present in subjects without the classic features of disease. In this way, our preliminary studies were associated with the development of new biochemical testing and algorithms based on the determination of serum cortisol (F) and cortisone (E) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0014"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0014" data-open="CIT0014" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">14</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0046"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0046" data-open="CIT0046" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">46</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0073"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0073" data-open="CIT0073" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">73</a>) by liquid chromatography (LC) tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS). Recently, we identified that 7.1% of a Chilean cohort (including hypertensive and normotensive subjects) fulfilled the criteria of a high F/E ratio (&gt;75th percentile) and low cortisone (&lt;25th percentile), defined as being suggestive of NC-AME. In this study, we observed a correlation between surrogate markers for impaired 11βHSD2 activity (high F/E ratio and low E levels), higher BP, lower renin activity, and increased urinary potassium excretion. Remarkably, a low cortisone level was the best predictor of MR activation, as assessed by renin activity and the fractional excretion of potassium (FEK). A positive association of cortisone and renin activity/potassium excretion was observed, even when adjusting for known modulators of renin, particularly age. Subjects with NC-AME have elevated microalbuminuria, PAI-1 and hsCRP, suggesting association of this phenotype with vascular and renal damage, and also a proinflammatory state (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F3"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F3" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 3</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">Similar to our findings, mouse models of 11βHSD2 enzyme deficiency have shown increased BP, atherogenesis progression, and proinflammatory changes in the heart and endothelium that are reduced by treatment with MR antagonists such as spironolactone or eplerenone (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0074 CIT0075 CIT0076 CIT0077"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0074 CIT0075 CIT0076 CIT0077" data-open="CIT0074 CIT0075 CIT0076 CIT0077" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">74–77</a>). Deuchar et al studied Apoe<sup>−/−</sup>/11β-HSD2<sup>−/−</sup> double-knockout (E/b2) mice and showed that the atherogenic effect of 11βHSD2 loss of function leads to remarkable atherogenesis progression and vascular damage associated with a proinflammatory process and further important protective effect seen by treatment with eplerenone (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0074"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0074" data-open="CIT0074" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">74</a>). Similarly, recent kidney-specific <em>Hsd11b2</em> knockout mice display high volume–low-renin hypertension and renal dysfunction, and this phenotype was reversed by spironolactone (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0078"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0078" data-open="CIT0078" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">78</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">In our previous studies, we considered only the serum F/E ratio without considering low serum cortisone values as a proxy of partial 11βHSD2 insufficiency, which could have given false positives by an elevation of cortisol with normal cortisone (as described in females using estrogen-based contraceptives), which is not enough to detect MR activation, which is a key factor in the diagnosis of AME (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0069"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0069" data-open="CIT0069" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">69</a>). This new definition for NC-AME, which is more accurate but less sensitive, may explain the lower frequency compared to our previous publications. High serum F/E ratios with concomitant low cortisone levels were more sensitive biomarkers than the urinary F/E ratio (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0079"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0079" data-open="CIT0079" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">79</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0080"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0080" data-open="CIT0080" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">80</a>) and the (THF + aTHF)/THE ratio (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0046"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0046" data-open="CIT0046" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">46</a>) to biochemically identify NC-AME subjects (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0014"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0014" data-open="CIT0014" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">14</a>)(<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-T1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="T1" data-open="T1" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">Table 1</a>). Serum determinations of morning cortisol and cortisone are associated with the morning adrenocorticotrophic hormone peak due to the circadian rhythm. High morning cortisol is a natural endogenous challenge for the 11βHSD2 enzyme in mineralocorticoid-sensitive tissues, where impaired 11βHSD2 should result in a decreased rate of conversion of active cortisol to cortisone.</p><p class="chapter-para">Although the clinical and biochemical features of AME syndrome have been extensively reported, the phenotypical presentation of heterozygous subjects has received little attention. Studies in mice have revealed the pathogenic potential of heterozygous mutations in the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene in the presence of a second hit, such as a high-sodium diet, which leads to a hypertensive phenotype. In 2011, Bailey et al (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0081"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0081" data-open="CIT0081" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">81</a>) reported that <em>Hsd11b2</em> heterozygous mice showed inverse relationships among 11βHSD2 activity, heart weight, and BP in a clinically relevant context. Moreover, these <em>Hsd11b2</em> heterozygous mice displayed sodium-sensitive AHT with normal clinical and biochemical profiles (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0081"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0081" data-open="CIT0081" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">81</a>). On a control diet, heterozygous mice displayed subtle signs of mineralocorticoid excess but had no derangements in BP or plasma electrolytes.</p><p class="chapter-para">The fact that up to one-third of hypertensives display low-renin hypertension suggests a systemic volume expansion, which responds to a natriuretic effect and hints that other etiologies, such as PA or NC-AME, can be detected or at least be suspected. These data are supported by Adlin et al (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0082"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0082" data-open="CIT0082" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">82</a>), who demonstrated in a Framingham-based sample of subjects with low-renin hypertension a bimodal aldosterone distribution, suggesting 2 different pathophysiological phenotypes: an aldosterone-dependent (such as occurs in PA) and, interestingly, a non-aldosterone-dependent (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0083"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0083" data-open="CIT0083" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">83</a>).</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922654 id="223922654" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s5>Etiology of NC-AME: Genetics, Epigenetics, and Chemical Inhibitors</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">The mild AME phenotype manifests as blood pressure elevation or mild hypertension later in life with rare or no electrolyte abnormalities, while the severe phenotype presents early in life with severe hypertension, failure to thrive, and early end–organ damage (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0084"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0084" data-open="CIT0084" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">84</a>). These phenotypic differences are likely related to differences in the level of enzyme expression and activity. Whereas 11βHSD2 enzyme expression is almost absent in the severe phenotype of AME; it is present in varying degrees in the mild form of AME (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0047"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0047" data-open="CIT0047" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">47</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0085"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0085" data-open="CIT0085" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">85</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0086"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0086" data-open="CIT0086" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">86</a>). However, the etiology of NC-AME is still unclear, and genetic variations account for only a small percentage of the occurrence of disease. Polymorphisms, variations in microsatellite regions, epigenetic modifications (DNA methylation, histone modifications, and microRNAs [miRNAs]) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>), and exogenous/endogenous chemical inhibitors (ie, licorice, grapefruit, or glycyrrhetinic acid; glycyrrhetinic acid-like factors [GALF]) have been postulated as factors that deregulate the expression and activity of <em>HSD11B2</em>.</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=223922656 id="223922656" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s6>Genetics</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">Nonclassic apparent mineralocorticoid is mainly related to deficiencies in the 11βHSD2 enzyme activity and genetic variations in the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene. Today, it is known that the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene is the only gene located in a region of 3 cM (centimorgans) on chromosome 16q22.1 that is associated with BP. There are more than 260 polymorphisms (SNPs) described in the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene, of which 166 have been studied. Of the 260 SNPs, 66 are located in coding regions, of which 35 are missense mutations, 10 are frame-shifted (Ins/Del) and 2 have a severe impairment of intron splicing (eg, exonic skipping) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>). Several association studies have been developed to identify associations between essential hypertension and <em>HSD11B2</em> polymorphisms. In a cohort of French hypertensive patients, Brand et al detected a polymorphic marker correlation between p.Glu178Glu (c.534G&gt;A; rs45483293) and essential hypertension (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0087"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0087" data-open="CIT0087" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">87</a>). However, Watson and colleagues studied 2 microsatellites and just found an association of D16S301 and hypertension in African Americans with end-stage renal disease (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0088"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0088" data-open="CIT0088" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">88</a>). Our group has studied the presence of polymorphisms in the adult Chilean population, especially polymorphisms in <em>HSD11B2</em>, the CA-repeat (D16S301), p.Glu178Glu (c.534G&gt;A; rs45483293), and p.Thr156Thr (c.468C&gt;A; rs5479), detecting an association of microsatellite (CA)n and c.534G&gt;A with low-renin hypertension. To date, few studies have performed sequencing analyses of the promoter and coding region of the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene in subjects not displaying the typical phenotype of classic AME. Tapia-Castillo et al (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0014"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0014" data-open="CIT0014" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">14</a>) identified 9 subjects with suggestive NC-AME phenotype, of whom 2 had the previously described heterozygous pathogenic variant R213C (p.Arg213Cys), which impairs the activity and stability of 11βHSD2 (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0046"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0046" data-open="CIT0046" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">46</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0058"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0058" data-open="CIT0058" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">58</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0089"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0089" data-open="CIT0089" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">89</a>). The R213C mutation in its recessive form is considered severe, since it disrupts critical hydrogen bonds in the central β-sheet of the structure that may change the protein folding and catalytic activity of 11βHSD2 (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0090"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0090" data-open="CIT0090" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">90</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0091"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0091" data-open="CIT0091" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">91</a>). In respect to the SNPs rs5479 (c.468C&gt;A; p.Thr156Thr) and rs45483293 (c.534G&gt;A; p.Glu178Glu), although they are related to hypertension (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0059"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0059" data-open="CIT0059" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">59</a>), they are synonymous variants of uncertain significance (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">The 11βHSD2 activity and expression can also be modified by different regulators, such as glucocorticoid receptor (GR) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0092"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0092" data-open="CIT0092" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">92</a>), RAC1-GTPase (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0093"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0093" data-open="CIT0093" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">93</a>), and SUMOylation modification (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0094"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0094" data-open="CIT0094" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">94</a>), among others. In 2019, Vitellius et al described subjects with pseudohypermineralocorticoidism with a similar phenotype to NC-AME. The authors indicate the heterozygous condition in GR leads to a decrease of activity of 11βHSD2 measured as a decrease in the E/F ratio (or an increase of the F/E ratio) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0092"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0092" data-open="CIT0092" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">92</a>). Lavall et al found that Rac1 GTPase regulates 11βHSD2 expression, MR activation, and MR-mediated profibrotic signaling (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0093"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0093" data-open="CIT0093" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">93</a>). Jimenez-Canino R et al (2017) found that SUMOylation of 11βHSD2 at residue K266 modulates cortisol-mediated MR nuclear translocation independently of effects on transactivation (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0094"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0094" data-open="CIT0094" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">94</a>). Zhu L et al (2016) found that activation of hedgehog signaling by a variety of approaches robustly induced 11βHSD2 expression as well as 11βHSD2 activity, especially in placental tissue (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0095"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0095" data-open="CIT0095" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">95</a>).</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=223922659 id="223922659" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s7>Epigenetics</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">A proposed mechanism involved in the negative regulation of the expression of the <em>HSD11B2</em> gene is epigenetics. Epigenetic changes are mainly DNA modifications that do not change the DNA sequence but can affect gene activity. Epigenetics encompasses several molecular processes that include nucleic acid methylation, histone modifications, and post-transcriptional control by noncoding RNAs (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0096 CIT0097 CIT0098"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0096 CIT0097 CIT0098" data-open="CIT0096 CIT0097 CIT0098" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">96–98</a>). These epigenetic modifications control gene expression by modulating the binding of transcriptional protein complexes and define which parts of the genome are either active or inactive (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0099"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0099" data-open="CIT0099" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">99</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0100"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0100" data-open="CIT0100" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">100</a>). DNA methylation is a potential epigenetic mechanism associated with cardiovascular diseases, including AHT, and may be linked to environmental nutritional factors (ie, salt intake, as a pathognomonic example) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0101 CIT0102 CIT0103 CIT0104 CIT0105"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0101 CIT0102 CIT0103 CIT0104 CIT0105" data-open="CIT0101 CIT0102 CIT0103 CIT0104 CIT0105" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">101–105</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">The <em>HSD11B2</em> promoter has a highly GC-rich core, which contains more than 80% GC, lacks a TATA-like element, and has 2 typical CpG islands (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F2" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 2</a>). This promoter configuration raises the possibility that CpG dinucleotide methylation may play a role in the cell type–specific and interindividual variable expression of <em>HSD11B2</em> (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0106"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0106" data-open="CIT0106" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">106</a>). In vitro and in vivo studies suggest a novel role of methylation of the promoter of <em>HSD11B2</em> (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0106 CIT0107 CIT0108"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0106 CIT0107 CIT0108" data-open="CIT0106 CIT0107 CIT0108" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">106–108</a>). Here, the CpG islands in the promoter region and the first exon of the gene have been found to be very methylated, which correlates with reduced gene expression. The demethylation enhances transcription and 11βHSD2 activity in vitro and, in rats, in vivo. Similarly, the methylation of artificial constructs of the <em>HSD11B2</em> promoter decreases transcriptional activity by preventing the binding of transcription factors relevant for its expression (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0106"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0106" data-open="CIT0106" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">106</a>), which supports CpG methylation as the most relevant epigenetic modification in the <em>HSD11B2</em> promoter (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0106"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0106" data-open="CIT0106" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">106</a>). A human study in essential hypertensive subjects by Friso et al reported epigenetic changes in the <em>HSD11B2</em> promoter in the peripheral leukocytes of essential hypertensive subjects challenged with low-dose corticosteroid therapy (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0109"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0109" data-open="CIT0109" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">109</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">In recent years, most of the studies addressing the role of the miRNA in hypertension have emerged as key players in transcription regulation in both healthy and disease states. They have been described as master gene regulators since they can regulate downstream gene expression by post-transcriptional mechanisms, specifically by binding to the 3′ untranslated region (UTR) of a messenger RNA (mRNA), leading to mRNA degradation or the repression of translation. The deregulation of miRNA expression is linked to many human pathological conditions; however, few studies have evaluated the relationship between miRNA expression in AHT (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0110"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0110" data-open="CIT0110" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">110</a>), the regulation of the MR pathway and the association with 11βHSD2 (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0111"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0111" data-open="CIT0111" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">111</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0112"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0112" data-open="CIT0112" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">112</a>). To date, 2 studies in animals have evaluated the relationship between the expression of miRNA and 11βHSD2 in vitro (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0111"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0111" data-open="CIT0111" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">111</a>) and provide the first evidence of the association between miRNA, 11βHSD2, and BP. In this sense, understanding the regulation of gene expression by miRNA is extremely critical for a better understanding of the pathophysiological mechanisms associated with NC-AME.</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=223922663 id="223922663" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s8>Exogenous and endogenous inhibitors of 11βHSD2</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">Several publications (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0113"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0113" data-open="CIT0113" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">113</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0114"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0114" data-open="CIT0114" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">114</a>) have addressed the effects of the inhibition of 11βHSD2 by environmental inhibitors, including licorice components, gossypol, phthalates, organotins, alkylphenols, perfluorinated substances, and carbenoxolone (a succinate derivative of glycyrrhetinic acid). Moreover, the 3α5α-tetrahydro-derivatives of several adrenal corticosteroid hormones, particularly corticosterone and 11-dehydrocorticosterone (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0115"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0115" data-open="CIT0115" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">115</a>), derivatives of progesterone and the adrenocorticosteroids (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0116 CIT0117 CIT0118"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0116 CIT0117 CIT0118" data-open="CIT0116 CIT0117 CIT0118" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">116–118</a>) 11β-OH-testosterone and 11-keto-testosterone, are potent inhibitors of 11βHSD2-dehydrogenase activity. A large proportion of patients with essential hypertension likely exhibit endogenous GALFs, particularly endogenous allo/3α5α-reduced pathway steroidal products. Earlier studies by Honour (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0119"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0119" data-open="CIT0119" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">119</a>) and Bokkenhauser et al (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0120"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0120" data-open="CIT0120" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">120</a>) indicated that a significant proportion of corticosterone and its 5α-ring A-reduced derivatives are excreted via the bile (in contrast to cortisol) in both humans and rodents. These steroids can then be 21-deoxygenated by microorganisms in the intestinal flora, yielding 11-oxygenated derivatives of progesterone and its 5α-tetrahydro-derivatives. The 5α-ring A-reduced derivatives of corticosterone and 11β-hydroxy-progesterone are even more potent inhibitors of the dehydrogenase activity of both isoforms of 11βHSD. A recent paper by Zhou et al (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0121"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0121" data-open="CIT0121" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">121</a>) described a group of environmental chemical inhibitors of 11βHSD2 as dithiocarbamates (DTCs) and organotins, butylated hydroxyanisole (BHA), perfluoroalkyl substances (PFASs), bisphenol A (BPA), zearalenone, 2-bis(p-hydroxyphenyl)-1,1,1-trichloroethane(HPTE), bortezomib (Velcade, PS-341), glycyrrhizin (GL) and its metabolites, and fluoxymesterone; others have described—also as chemical inhibitors of 11βHSD2—heavy metals (ie, lead) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0122"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0122" data-open="CIT0122" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">122</a>) and fungicides (ie, poconazole) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0121"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0121" data-open="CIT0121" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">121</a>).</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=223922665 id="223922665" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s9>Salt sensitivity associated with <em>HSD11B2</em> gene expression</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">Salt sensitivity is also an independent risk factor for adverse cardiovascular events in normotensive individuals (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0126"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0126" data-open="CIT0126" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">126</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0123"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0123" data-open="CIT0123" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">123</a>) and is a negative prognostic indicator for clinical progression toward hypertension, microalbuminuria, and endothelial dysfunction. An increased sodium appetite has been reported in AME (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0124"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0124" data-open="CIT0124" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">124</a>), and loss-of-function variants are positively associated with sodium intake in the general population (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0108"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0108" data-open="CIT0108" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">108</a>). <em>Hsd11b2</em> heterozygous null mice, which have normal basal BP, cannot efficiently excrete a sodium load associated with a dysregulation of ENaC activity and are salt sensitive (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0125"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0125" data-open="CIT0125" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">125</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0126"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0126" data-open="CIT0126" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">126</a>). Studies in humans report that 11βHSD2 in the brain may contribute to abnormal sodium homeostasis. Researchers suggest that 11βHSD2-positive neurons integrate sodium appetite and salt sensitivity through an MR-dependent pathway. Bailey and colleagues found that brain-specific knockout of this gene does not affect basal BP in mice but results in a 3-fold increase in salt appetite compared with littermate controls and induces a salt sensitivity of BP (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0127"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0127" data-open="CIT0127" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">127</a>). These results indicate that NC-AME subjects with an associated first hit from pathogenic variations or epigenetic modifications in <em>HSD11B2</em> could increase either sodium renal reabsorption or sodium appetite, adding another risk factor for hypertension morbidities and cardiovascular disease. The early identification of subjects with NC-AME should support further clinical, genetic, and nutritional advice, since a second hit can lead to BP elevation and further AHT (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0128"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0128" data-open="CIT0128" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">128</a>).</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922667 id="223922667" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s10>Treatment</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Treatment for AME must be started at diagnosis, and sustained and monitored. Mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists at age-appropriate doses are mandatory: relatively low doses are sufficient, given the side effects (spironolactone) or cost (eplerenone), with amiloride and calcium channel blockers occasionally required to normalize BP. In addition, patients can also be treated with glucocorticoids that have a long half-life, such as dexamethasone or betamethasone, decreasing ACTH levels and endogenous cortisol. Patients with normotensive NC-AME should have medical surveillance and a low-sodium/high-potassium diet recommendation. Subjects with NC-AME and elevated BP, or those who are hypertensive should be treated with low-dose MR antagonists and potassium supplementation to control BP and BP-independent damage occurring in other tissues, such as the heart and blood vessels, among others, by unregulated activation of MR (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-F1"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="F1" class="link xref-fig">Fig. 1</a>). According to Funder (2017), treatment of mild AME or NC-AME with elevated BP should be straightforward (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0129"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0129" data-open="CIT0129" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">129</a>) with low-dose spironolactone (12.5–25 mg/day) (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0130"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0130" data-open="CIT0130" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">130</a>) or eplerenone (25–50 mg/day) with the intention to correct BP and improve renin levels. Despite that there is no prospective data in NC-AME, inference from PA studies support the concept of improved renin levels as a proxy of MR inactivation (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0131"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0131" data-open="CIT0131" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">131</a>). Hundemer et al showed that MR antagonist treatment addressed to normalize renin levels leads to better cardiovascular outcomes (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-CIT0131"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="CIT0131" data-open="CIT0131" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">131</a>).</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922669 id="223922669" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=s11>Conclusion</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Classic AME is a relatively rare disorder characterized by a severe low-renin hypertensive phenotype in childhood. The diagnosis of AME requires a demonstration of a biallelic deficiency of <em>HSD11B2</em> and very low 11βHSD2 enzyme activity. The treatment of AME includes MR antagonists, glucocorticoid and potassium supplementation, and additional antihypertensive therapy. Nonclassic apparent mineralocorticoid excess differs from classic AME syndrome because it has a milder phenotype, is diagnosed later in life in adolescents and adults, and can be observed even in normotensive subjects. The NC-AME phenotype is mainly associated to genetic variants in <em>HSD11B2</em> and epigenetic modifications that, along with a second hit, are able to impair the proper cortisol metabolism. Since NC-AME is associated to low-renin phenotype and to an increased cardiovascular risk due to MR activation, these subjects are candidates to be treated with MR antagonists to improve BP and end-organ damage, and modulate renin levels.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922671 id="223922671" class="backacknowledgements-title js-splitscreen-backacknowledgements-title" data-legacy-id=a1>Acknowledgments</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">We acknowledge all our patients and researchers that supported previous studies associated with this manuscript.</p><p class="chapter-para"><strong><em>Financial Support:</em></strong> Grants Fondo Nacional de Desarrollo Científico y Tecnológico (CONICYT) 1150437, 1160695, 1190250, and 1190419; Fondo Nacional de Equipamiento Científico y Tecnológico (CONICYT) EQM150023; Millennium Institute on Immunology and Immunotherapy (IMII) P09/16-F (ICM-MINECON); Corporación de Fomento de la Producción &amp; Biomedical Research Consortium 13CTI-21526-P1; Minisabatico 2017 (DIDEMUC) &amp; Pasantías Breves de Investigación VRI 2018 PBI-1811 (to CAC); SOCHED 2019-09 and CETREN-UC.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922675 id="223922675" class="backsection-title js-splitscreen-backsection-title" data-legacy-id=s0105>Additional Information</h2>
<p class="chapter-para"><strong><em>Disclosure Summary:</em></strong> The authors have nothing to disclose.</p><p class="chapter-para"><strong><em>Data Availability:</em></strong> Data sharing is not applicable to this article as no datasets were generated or analyzed during the current study.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=223922678 id="223922678" class="backreferences-title js-splitscreen-backreferences-title" data-legacy-id=r1>References</h2>
